U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
NCHS Website 
Data users can obtain the latest information about the National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) by periodically 
checking our website: https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis.htm 
The website features downloadable data and documentation for the 2025 NHIS and previous years, as well as 
important information about any modifications or updates to the data or documentation. Published reports 
from previous years’ surveys are also available, as are updates about future surveys and datasets. Data files and 
documentation can be found at: https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/documentation/index.html 
 
 
 
NHIS Electronic Mail List 
 
Data users are encouraged to join the NHIS Listserv, an electronic mailing list. The Listserv is made up of over 
3,200 NHIS data users located around the world who receive news about NHIS surveys (e.g., new releases of 
data or modifications to existing data), and related products. To join, go to 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/products/nchs_listservs.htm, and select “National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) 
Researchers” as one of your options, or select “subscribe now” at the bottom of the NHIS annual release page.  
 
 
 
Questions about NHIS? 
 
The staff of the Division of Health Interview Statistics at the National Center for Health Statistics respond to data 
users’ questions about NHIS. Users may e-mail us at nhislist@cdc.gov. A response may take 1-2 business days. 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 8 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Guidelines for Citation of Data Source 
 
With the goal of mutual benefit, the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS) requests that recipients of NHIS 
data files cooperate in certain actions related to their use. 
 
Any published material derived from the 2025 NHIS data should acknowledge “National Center for Health 
Statistics, National Health Interview Survey” as the original source. The full spelling of the source without the 
use of acronyms is preferred.  
 
The suggested citation to appear at the bottom of all tables and graphs is as follows: 
 
Data Source: National Center for Health Statistics, National Health Interview Survey, 2025. 
In a bibliography, the suggested citation for this document is: 
National Center for Health Statistics. National Health Interview Survey, 2025 survey description. 2026. 
Available from: 
https://ftp.cdc.gov/pub/Health_Statistics/NCHS/Dataset_Documentation/NHIS/2025/srvydesc-508.pdf 
 
 
The suggested citation for 2025 NHIS survey data and other documentation is: 
 
National Center for Health Statistics. National Health Interview Survey, 2025. Public-use data file and 
documentation. https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/documentation/index.html. 2026. 
 
The published material should also include a disclaimer that credits the author’s analyses, interpretations, and 
conclusions to the author (recipient of the data file) and not to NCHS, which is responsible only for the initial 
data. Users who wish to publish a technical description of the data should make a reasonable effort to ensure 
that the description is consistent with that published by NCHS. 
 
NHIS questionnaires are in the public domain and no permission is required to use them. Citation as to source, 
however, is appreciated. 
 
Information on how to cite NCHS publications and electronic media is available at: 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/products/citations.htm.

U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Data User Agreement 
 
Please Read Carefully Before Using the National Health Interview Survey 
 
The National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) is conducted by the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS), 
Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). 
 
NCHS, CDC conducts statistical and epidemiological activities under the authority granted by the Public Health 
Service Act (42 U.S.C. § 242k). NCHS survey data such as NHIS are protected by Federal confidentiality laws 
including Section 308(d) Public Health Service Act [42 U.S.C. 242m(d)] and the Confidential Information 
Protection and Statistical Efficiency Act (CIPSEA) of 2018 (44 U.S.C. 3561-3583). These confidentiality laws state 
the data collected by NCHS may be used only for statistical reporting and analysis. Any effort to determine the 
identity of individuals and establishments violates the assurances of confidentiality provided by federal law. 
 
 
 
Terms and Conditions 
 
NCHS does all it can to assure that the identity of individuals and establishments cannot be disclosed. All direct 
identifiers, as well as any characteristics that might lead to identification, are omitted from the dataset. Any 
intentional identification or disclosure of an individual or establishment violates the assurances of confidentiality 
given to the providers of the information. Therefore, users will: 
 
• Use the data in this dataset for statistical reporting and analysis only. 
• Make no attempt to learn the identity of any person or establishment included in these data. 
• Not link this dataset with individually identifiable data from other NCHS or non-NCHS datasets. 
• Not engage in any efforts to assess disclosure methodologies applied to protect individuals and 
establishments or any research on methods of re-identification of individuals and establishments. 
 
By using these data, you signify your agreement to comply with the above-stated statutorily based requirements. 
 
 
 
Sanctions for Violating NCHS Data Use Agreement 
 
Anyone willfully disclosing any information that  could identify a person or establishment in any manner to a  
person or agency not entitled to receive it, shall be guilty of a class E felony and imprisoned for not more than 5 
years, or fined not more than $250,000, or both. 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 10 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
What’s New in 2025? 
 
• Starting in 2025, a modified sample design based on the 2020 Census that uses a stratified, two-stage 
sample was implemented. Primary sampling units (PSUs) were selected during the first stage with 
household units drawn annually at the second stage. This design incorporates the latest decennial census 
population counts and uses enhanced sample design requirements and improvements. Notably, it provides 
a better balance between interviewer or field representative (FR) travel costs and the geographic clustering 
of sampled households, while enabling the production of more reliable state-level estimates.  
 
• Analysts interested in combining or pooling public-use NHIS data files from 2025 and 2024 (or prior years 
since 2019) do not need to account for the different sample design in variance estimation. Analysts using 
the restricted variance estimation variables through the Research Data Center should differentiate the 
strata values between 2025 and prior years. Options include adding or multiplying one of the annual strata 
by a constant value to make the strata values distinct between 2025 and the prior years, or by specifying 
two designs when declaring the variance variables in the analysis.  
 
• Due to the lapse in appropriations between October 1 and November 12 of 2025, data collection by the 
U.S. Census Bureau was paused between October 1 and November 16, with NHIS data collection restarting 
on November 18. This pause in operations resulted in a reduction in the number of interviews completed in 
2025 and slight delays in the processing of data that were collected. Specific data collection and data file 
impacts are described below: 
o The interview period for the October, November, and December sample shifted. FRs were instructed to 
work October cases from November 18 to December 7, November cases from December 1 to January 4, 
and December case from January 1 to January 31, 2026. This resulted in a slight overlap in October and 
November sample interview periods. 
o Data collected from January 1 to January 31, 2026, are excluded from 2025 annual file. 
o The month of interview (INTV_MON2) and quarter of interview (INTV_QRT2) variables have been 
revised to reflect the shift and overlap in interview periods. October and November sample cases are 
coded as “99” in INTV_MON2 and coded as “9” in INTV_QRT2. 
o In addition to data collection changes due to a lapse in appropriations, data collection was also 
impacted by limitations on FRs' travel, hiring, and retention in 2025. 
 
• Continuing since 2019, the NHIS questionnaire includes four types of content: 1) Annual core is content that 
is included in the NHIS every year; 2) Rotating core is scheduled to appear on a periodic basis of every other 
year, one out of every three years, or two out of every three years; 3) Sponsored content includes 
questions that are funded by other agencies; and 4) Emerging content is content that is sponsored by NCHS 
on new health topics. A visualization that summarizes 2019–2027 NHIS content by year is available for the 
Sample Adult (https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/nhis/Sample-Adult-Questionnaire.pdf) and the Sample Child 
(https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/data/nhis/Sample-Child-Questionnaire.pdf). 
 
• Annual core content remains largely the same in 2025 except: 
o The “read if necessary” text to the question “Is everyone in this household a member of the same 
family?” in the FAM section of the Roster was reworded to make it clear that respondents should 
include unmarried couples living together as family. 
o Six new questions were added to the sample adult and sample child health insurance section (INS) in 
Quarter 4 to capture changes to a person’s health care coverage over the past 12 months. The six 
questions ask if a person had health insurance purchased through an employer, exchange or 
marketplace such as Healthcare.gov, or had Medicaid, medical assistance, or coverage through the 
Children’s Health Insurance Program in the past 12 months. Data for these questions are only available 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 11 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
through the Research Data Center. 
 
• Additional information on the 2025 rotating, sponsored and emerging content is described below: 
 
Sample Adult Rotating Core 
 
Aspirin use*, chronic pain, dental care, hysterectomy, physical and other therapeutic care, and 
preventive screening (clinical checks for blood pressure, cholesterol, and blood glucose, and screening 
for breast, cervical, colorectal** cancer)— All previously fielded in 2023. 
 
GAD-7 anxiety scale and PHQ-8 depression scale— All previously fielded in 2022.  
 
*In 2025, only two of the four low-dose aspirin questions fielded in the previous rotations were included.   
**In 2025, the age of Sample Adults asked about colorectal cancer screening was lowered to 35 years 
from age 40 years used in the previous rotations.  
 
Sample Child Rotating Core 
 
Physical and other therapeutic care and stressful life events — All previously fielded in 2023. 
 
Strength and difficulties questionnaire (SDQ) — Previously fielded in 2022. 
 
Sample Adult Sponsored Content 
 
Age of disability onset, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, caregiving, chronic pain management, 
diabetes distress, epilepsy, immunization (COVID-19, respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), shingles, and 
tetanus, diphtheria, and acellular pertussis (Tdap)), insulin use, life satisfaction, Long COVID, social 
support and loneliness, menopausal symptoms, non-cigarette tobacco products, non-insulin injectable 
medications to lower blood sugar or lose weight, pregnancy in the past 12 months that ended in live 
birth, preventive screening (colon, prostate, breast, and cervical cancer), social connectedness and 
isolation, traumatic brain injury, voice, swallowing, speech, and language communication disorders, and 
whole person health.  
 
Sample Child Sponsored Content 
 
COVID-19 vaccination, Long COVID, traumatic brain injury, and voice, swallowing, speech, and language 
communication disorders. 
 
Sample Adult Emerging Content 
 
Civic engagement, internet access and health information technology, language spoken at home, social 
and emotional parenting support, and volunteer activities.

U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
About NHIS 
 
NHIS is the principal source of information on the health of the civilian noninstitutionalized population of the 
United States and is one of the major data collection programs of the NCHS. The National Health Survey Act of 
1956 provided for a continuing survey and special studies to secure accurate and current statistical information 
on the amount, distribution, and effects of illness and disability in the United States and the services rendered 
for or because of such conditions. The survey referred to in the Act, now called the National Health Interview 
Survey, was initiated in July 1957. Since 1960, the survey has been conducted by NCHS, which was formed when 
the National Health Survey and the National Vital Statistics Division were combined. 
 
The main objective of the NHIS is to monitor the health of the United States population through the collection 
and analysis of data on a broad range of health topics. A major strength of this survey lies in the ability to 
categorize these health characteristics by many demographic and socioeconomic characteristics. 
 
NHIS data are used widely throughout the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) to monitor trends in 
illness and disability and to track progress toward achieving national health objectives. The data are also used by 
the public health research community for epidemiologic and policy analysis of such timely issues as 
characterizing those with various health problems, determining barriers to accessing and using appropriate 
health care, and evaluating Federal health programs. 
 
Since 1957, the content of the survey has been updated about every 10–15 years to incorporate advances in 
survey methodology and coverage of health topics. In January 2019, NHIS launched a redesigned content and 
structure that differs from the 1997–2018 NHIS. 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 13 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Overview of 2025 Survey Methods 
 
NHIS is a nationally representative household survey of the U.S. civilian noninstitutionalized population. It is 
conducted continuously throughout the year by the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS). However, due 
to a lapse in federal appropriations, NHIS data were not collected from October 1 to November 16, 2025. 
Interviews are typically initiated in respondents’ homes, but follow-ups to complete interviews may be 
conducted over the telephone. In 2025, 53.1% of the Sample Adult interviews and 54.3% of the Sample Child 
interviews were conducted at least partially by telephone. 
 
Information about the Sample Adult is self-reported, unless physically or mentally unable to do so (and then a 
knowledgeable proxy can answer for the Sample Adult). Information about the Sample Child is collected from a 
parent or adult who is knowledgeable about and responsible for the health care of the Sample Child. In 2025, 
there were 24,215 Sample Adult interviews and 6,049 Sample Child interviews. Starting in 2025 (and available 
retrospectively from 2019-2024), weighted response rates are reported. In 2025, the weighted final Sample 
Adult response rate was 41.3% and the weighted final Sample Child response rate was 42.0%. The response 
rates in 2025 were impacted by the cessation of data collection due to the lapse in federal appropriations, the 
overall challenges hiring and retaining field representatives (FR), and the moratorium on federal travel that 
prevented FRs’ travel to areas with low field coverage.  
 
NHIS includes annual content that appears on the survey every year. The survey also includes rotating core 
content, sponsored content, and emerging content that appears periodically. Visit 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/about/2019-questionnaire-redesign.html for a description of content in any 
given year.  
 
For more information about NHIS, visit: https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/index.html. An itemized checklist is 
also available that summarizes the information necessary for researchers to implement the Preferred Reporting 
Items for Complex Sample Survey Analysis (PRICSSA) reporting guidelines (Available from: 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/documentation/2025-nhis.html).  
 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 14 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
NHIS Methods 
 
I. Sample Design 
 
NHIS is a cross-sectional household interview survey. The target population for the NHIS is the civilian 
noninstitutionalized population residing within the 50 states and the District of Columbia at the time of the 
interview. The NHIS universe includes residents of households and noninstitutional group quarters (e.g., 
homeless shelters, rooming houses, and group homes). Persons residing temporarily in student dormitories or 
temporary housing are sampled within the households where they usually reside (e.g., their parents’ 
household). Persons excluded from the universe are those with no fixed household address (e.g., homeless and 
transient persons not residing in shelters), active duty military personnel and civilians living on military bases, 
persons in long-term care institutions (e.g., nursing homes for the elderly, hospitals for the chronically ill or 
physically or intellectually disabled, and wards for abused or neglected children), persons in correctional 
facilities (e.g., prisons or jails, juvenile detention centers, and halfway houses), and U.S. nationals living in foreign 
countries. While active-duty Armed Forces personnel are not sampled for inclusion in the survey, any civilians 
residing with Armed Forces personnel in non-military housing are eligible to be sampled. 
 
NHIS is typically initiated in a face-to-face interview format. However, the costs of interviewing a large simple 
random sample of households and noninstitutional group quarters would be prohibitive, and randomly sampled 
dwelling units would be too dispersed throughout the nation for cost-effective interviewing. To keep survey 
operations manageable, cost-effective, and timely, the NHIS uses geographically clustered sampling techniques 
to select the sample of dwelling units for the NHIS. The sample is designed in such a way that each month’s 
sample is nationally representative. Data collection on the NHIS is continuous, i.e., from January to December 
each year. 
 
Every ten years, the sample design for the NHIS is redrawn to align with updated population counts from the 
most recent decennial census. This is also an opportunity to change sampling requirements and to incorporate 
design improvements. The 2025 NHIS sample redesign, which aligns with the 2020 decennial census, was 
implemented in 2025 and was driven by the need to have a nationally representative sample providing coverage 
in all 50 states and the District of Columbia (DC). Additionally, a new requirement was to include the ability to 
generate state-level estimates based on three years of pooled data. The redesigned sample retained the ability 
to pool data over time. To meet these requirements, the 2025 NHIS sample design uses a stratified, two-stage 
sample. The first stage PSUs were selected at the start of the design, while the second stage household units are 
selected on an annual basis.  
 
The process of deﬁning PSUs involves combining all the counties and county-equivalents in the nation into over 
1500 PSUs. This nationwide universe of PSUs is the ﬁrst-stage sampling frame used when selecting sample PSUs 
for the design. The 2025 NHIS design implements a novel method of cycling non-self-representing (NSR) PSUs in 
which sample NSR PSUs are cycled in and out of sample during the ten-year design period. Cycling ensures more 
geographic dispersion and degrees of freedom for each of the 50 states and D.C. when pooling three years of 
data. NSR PSUs remain in sample for two or three consecutive years before rotating out. The NSR PSUs staying in 
sample for at least two years helps to ease the burden on field operations in managing staff. Highly populated 
PSUs are designated as self-representing (SR) and are in-sample every year of the design. The sample size is set 
to annually achieve 30,000 complete adult interviews. While the baseline goal is to achieve 30,000 complete 
adult interviews each year, budget constraints can require a reduction of this goal in a particular year. This was 
the case in 2025, the first year of this sample design. The minimum sample size in each state is set to achieve the 
estimated number of complete adult interviews necessary to ensure that an estimate of approximately 10 
percent has a 95 percent confidence interval width no larger than 1.3 times its proportion (assuming a design 
effect of three). 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 15 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
The current design also marks the beginning of annual sampling. In previous designs, the bulk of the sample was 
selected at the start of the ten-year period, with new growth added over time. Beginning with units interviewed 
in the field in 2025, all housing units are now selected annually in the within-PSU sampling processes. Similar to 
the 2010 NHIS sample design, the majority of the NHIS sampling frame is based on an address list provided by a 
vendor. This portion of the frame is referred to as the Unit Frame. The remainder of the NHIS sample frame is 
referred to as the Area Frame and is composed of the results of listings carried out by FRs. For the 2025 NHIS 
sample, 5.5% of the sampled housing units were from the area frame and 94.5% of the sampled housing unit 
were from the unit frame. A report on the new NHIS sample design starting in 2025 is forthcoming.  
 
 
 
II. Interviewing Procedures 
 
Data Collection 
 
The U.S. Census Bureau, under a contractual agreement, is the data collection agent for the National Health 
Interview Survey. Typically, NHIS data are collected continuously throughout the year by Census FRs. 
 
2025  
Due to a lapse in federal appropriations, NHIS data were not collected from October 1 to November 16, 
2025. The 2025 data collection also faced unique challenges in addition to the cessation of data 
collection. Restrictions in federal hiring and staff retention led to a severe shortage in FRs which 
impacted response rates. The moratorium on federal travel also meant that FRs could not travel to 
areas with inadequate numbers of FRs to complete interviews.  
 
In 2025, 636 FRs conducted NHIS interviews nationwide. FRs are trained and directed by health survey 
supervisors in the U.S. Census Bureau Regional Offices. FRs are observed by supervisors periodically, and their 
work is monitored by the Census Bureau’s PANDA system, a performance and data analysis program that 
provides monthly checks on response rates, completion rates, item response times, item nonresponse, 
telephone usage rates, and other data quality indicators. FR performance is also assessed with the Field Quality 
Monitoring (FQM) system, a near real-time monitoring that integrates performance metrics across all surveys 
worked by an FR. Metrics assessed include patterns in the rate of vacant and usual residence elsewhere, actual 
versus billed distance between home and personal visits, number of personal visit attempts per day, and 
variation in the days and times of attempts. The supervisors responsible for the NHIS are career Civil Service 
employees who are selected through an examination and testing process. FRs receive thorough refresher 
training annually and other training during the year in basic interviewing procedures and in the concepts and 
procedures unique to the NHIS. 
 
Each household address selected for participation in the NHIS is mailed a letter prior to the FR’s visit. The “Advance 
letter” is mailed one week prior to the start of the interview period (one week before the 1st of the month) with 
the goal that it might be fresh on people’s mind when the FR makes contact the first few days of the month. This 
“Advance letter” contains information about the purpose of the NHIS and the amount of time the interview will 
require, and it assures potential respondents that participation in the NHIS is voluntary. It also informs respondents 
that the information they provide is protected by law and details how the information will be used. When the FR 
arrives at the household address, he/she provides another copy of the “Advance letter” to each respondent and 
obtains verbal consent for survey participation. A copy of the current “Advance letter” and other NHIS materials 
available for distribution by FRs in the field are available at the NHIS participants’ page: 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis-participants/what-to-expect/index.html. 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 16 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
The NHIS is conducted using computer-assisted personal interviewing (CAPI). The CAPI data collection method 
employs Blaise computer software that presents questions on computer screens to each FR. The instrument guides 
the FR through the questionnaire, automatically routing the FR to appropriate questions based on answers to 
previous questions. FRs are trained to read the questions as written in the instrument and probe if necessary. Unless 
noted in the introduction to the question or in the question text, “refuse” and “don’t know” responses are not read to 
respondent but accepted as a response to any survey question. FRs enter survey responses directly into the 
computer, and the CAPI program determines if the selected response is within an allowable range, checks it for 
consistency against some of the other data collected during the interview, and saves the responses into a survey 
data file. The computer contains help facilities to aid FRs in administering the CAPI questionnaire. This data 
collection technology reduces the time required for transferring, processing, and releasing data, and it ensures the 
accurate flow of the questionnaire. 
 
Typically, face-to-face interviews are initiated in respondents’ homes, but follow-ups to complete interviews 
may be conducted over the telephone. A telephone interview may also be conducted when the respondent 
requests a telephone interview or when road conditions or travel distances would make it difficult to schedule a 
personal visit before the required completion date. 
 
Due to the closure of the federal government between October 1 and November 12 of 2025, data collection by 
the U.S. Census Bureau was paused between October 1 and November 16. The October NHIS sample was fielded 
between November 18 and December 7, and the November NHIS sample was fielded between December 1 and 
January 4 of 2026. Data collected using the October and November samples were included in the 2025 data files. 
The December NHIS sample was fielded between January 1
st and 31st. Data collected using the December sample 
was not included in the 2025 data files. Therefore, the 2025 NHIS data files contain data from 11 months of 
sample. Variables for month of interview and quarter of interview were revised to categorize sample months 10 
and 11 as 99 and quarter 4 as 9 to differentiate these variables from previous years. Weights were adjusted to 
account for 11 months of sample— see Weighting section for more information 
 
 
 
Field Results 
 
In 2025, 53.1% of the Sample Adult interviews and 54.3% of the Sample Child interviews were conducted at 
least partially by telephone. This is consistent with the percentage of telephone interviews that were conducted 
at least partially by telephone in 2024 (53.2% for Sample Adult interviews and 54.8% for Sample Child 
interviews), but still considerably higher than interviews in 2019 (pre-pandemic) for both the Sample Adult 
(34.3%) and Sample Child (31.7%). 
 
The Paradata data file, available with the annual data release, has information collected about the interview 
process.  
 
 
 
Rostering and Respondents 
 
For the Household Roster section of the questionnaire, any responsible household member age 18 years and 
older is identified to act as the “Household Respondent.” The Household Respondent provides names, age, sex, 
race, and ethnicity for all household members. The highest level of education completed, and active military 
status is asked for all adult household members age 18 years and older. In addition to collecting this basic 
demographic information, the Household Roster interview also identifies whether all persons in the household 
are members of the same or different family. Note that in a multi-family household, a single “Household 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 17 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Respondent” provides household information for all families. 
 
NHIS has consistently defined a family as an individual or a group of two or more people residing together who 
are related by birth, marriage, or adoption. A family additionally includes any unrelated children who are cared 
for by the family (such as foster children) and any unmarried cohabiting partners and their children. After the 
Household Roster is completed, data are collected on one adult and child per household. 
 
A “Sample Adult” is randomly selected by the computer from each household with at least one household 
member age 18 years and older and is asked more detailed health related questions. The Sample Adult 
responds for him/herself to the questions in that section unless he/she is physically or mentally unable to do so, 
in which case a knowledgeable proxy may answer for the Sample Adult. Students age 18 and older living away at 
college, trade, or commercial schools in on-campus housing are eligible to be interviewed in the location they 
consider to be their usual residence, such as their parent’s or other family member’s household. Students living 
away at school or college in off-campus housing will not be included as members of the household, since they 
could be sampled at their off-campus location. 
 
A “Sample Child” is randomly selected by the computer from each household with at least one child 17 years of 
age or younger. An adult respondent who was previously indicated to be knowledgeable and responsible for the 
Sample Child’s health is asked questions about that child. In 2025, 94.6% of the Sample Child respondents were 
the child’s parent, either a biological, adoptive, or stepparent, while 3.6% were a grandparent, 0.3% were an 
aunt or uncle, 0.6% were an adult sibling, 0.8% were another relative or other non-relative, and 0.05% were not 
ascertained. For each sampled household address, FRs also maintain electronic documentation about the NHIS 
interview process, including contact attempts, observed characteristics about the exterior of the sample unit or 
vicinity, and descriptive information about the interview outcome. 
 
 
 
Confidentiality 
 
All information collected by the NHIS that would permit identification of the individual is held strictly confidential, 
seen only by persons who work on the NHIS (including related studies carried out by the Public Health Service) with 
a need to know, and such information is not disclosed or released to anyone for any other purpose without the 
consent of the respondent. NCHS must adhere to Section 308(d) of the Public Health Service Act (42 U.S.C. 
242m(d)), which forbids the disclosure of any information that may compromise the confidentiality promised to 
survey respondents. In addition, confidentiality protections are also mandated by the Privacy Act of 1974 (5 U.S.C. § 
552a), and the
 Confidential Information Protection and Statistical Efficiency Act of 2018 (44 U.S.C. 3561-3583).  In 
addition to the above cited laws, NCHS complies with the Federal Cybersecurity Enhancement Act of 2015 (6 U.S.C. 
§§ 151 and 151 note) which protects Federal information systems from cybersecurity risks by screening their 
networks. 
 
Further information about data collection procedures is available in the National Health Interview Survey: 2025 CAPI 
Manual for NHIS Field Representatives available on the NHIS website,  
https://ftp.cdc.gov/pub/Health_Statistics/NCHS/Survey_Questionnaires/NHIS/2025/2025-NHIS-CAPI-Manual.pdf . 
 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 18 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
III. NHIS Design 
 
In 2019, the structure and content of the NHIS questionnaire was redesigned to better meet the needs of data 
users, CDC, and HHS. The goals of the redesign were to reduce respondent burden by shortening the length of 
the questionnaire, harmonize overlapping content with other federal health surveys, establish a long-term 
structure of ongoing and periodic topics, and incorporate advances in survey methodology and measurement. 
For more information about the 2019 Questionnaire Redesign, visit: 
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/about/2019-questionnaire-redesign.html. For information about the previous 
NHIS design conducted during 1997–2018, refer to year-specific NHIS documentation: 
https://archive.cdc.gov/www_cdc_gov/nchs/nhis/1997-2018.htm 
 
 
 
Structure of NHIS 
 
The redesigned NHIS questionnaire consists of three modules: (1) Household Roster; (2) Sample Adult Interview; 
and (3) Sample Child Interview. Following a brief household rostering interview that collects basic demographics of 
everyone who usually lives or stays in the household and identifies which members of the household are in the 
same or a separate family, one “Sample Adult” age 18 years and older and one “Sample Child” age 17 years or 
younger (if any children live in the household) are randomly selected from each household. Information about the 
household members is collected from a person age 18 years and older residing at the sample address. Information 
about the Sample Adult is collected from the Sample Adult themself unless they are physically or mentally unable to 
do so, in which case a knowledgeable proxy can answer for the Sample Adult. Information about the Sample Child is 
collected from a parent or adult who is knowledgeable and responsible for the health care of the Sample Child. The 
respondent for the Household Roster, Sample Adult and Sample Child modules may or may not be the same 
person.   
 
Figure 1 (on the next page) illustrates the interviewing flow of the Household Roster, Sample Adult and Sample Child 
interviews or modules in the NHIS. The Sample Adult and Sample Child may be part of the same family or be part of 
different families in the household. Family-level content (e.g., family income, food insecurity) is collected in the 
Sample Adult or Sample Child questionnaire module.  
 
 
 
Content of the Household Roster 
 
In the Household Roster, an adult (age 18 years and over) living in the household provides basic information about 
themselves and other people living in the household. The name, age, sex, race, and ethnicity of everyone in the 
household are collected. Additionally, the parents of all children are identified. The instrument then randomly 
selects one adult (Sample Adult) and one child (Sample Child), if any children live in the household, to be given 
follow-up questions. The Sample Adult is selected randomly among persons age 18 years and older living in the 
household, and the Sample Child is selected randomly among those age 17 years or younger. Questions are asked 
to determine who is in the family of the Sample Adult and Sample Child. The Sample Adult and Sample Child do not 
need to be in the same family. No health information is collected in this section. When the Household Roster is 
complete, the FRs can then proceed with the Sample Adult or Sample Child interview (if a child lives in the 
household). 
 
Figure 2 (on the next page) illustrates how topics or content in the NHIS are organized by interviewing modules (i.e., 
Household Roster, Sample Adult and Sample Child). 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 19 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Figure 1. NHIS Structure 
 
 
 
 
In households where there is an eligible Sample Adult and Sample Child, either the Sample Adult or Sample Child 
interview is administered first. Once both the Sample Adult and Sample Child interviews have been completed 
the interview is complete.  
 
The NHIS is a survey of the civilian noninstitutionalized population, and active-duty military personnel are not 
included. In the rare case where a child lives in a household consisting of only active-duty military personnel, 
the Household Roster would be completed followed by a Sample Child interview. 
 
 
 
Content of the Sample Child Interview 
 
An adult knowledgeable and responsible for the health of the child is asked a set of questions about the Sample 
Child. Some questions asked of the Sample Child vary by age, as younger and older children have different health 
needs. Additional demographic information is also collected about the child and their family.

Content of the Sample Adult Interview 
 
The Sample Adult is asked a series of health questions about themselves. Some questions vary by age or sex of 
the Sample Adult, but most are the same for all Sample Adults. Additional demographic information is also 
collected about the Sample Adult and their family. 
 
 
 
Types of Questions 
 
The redesigned NHIS incorporates a long- term structure of fixed and periodic content. The long -term structure 
for the Sample Adult and Sample Child questionnaires organizes question topics by year and by type of content 
for the survey years 2019–2027. Additional information about periodicity of question topics for 2019-2027, see,  
https://www.cdc.gov/nchs/nhis/about/2019-questionnaire-redesign.html. There are four types of content: (1) 
Annual core; (2) Rotating core; (3) Sponsored content; and (4) Emerging topics. 
 
Annual core are consistent questions that are asked every year. 
 
Rotating core are questions that are asked some, but not all years. These questions are scheduled to 
appear on a periodic basis of every other year, one out of every three years, or two out of every 
three years. 
 
Sponsored content are questions funded by other federal agencies or other centers within CDC about 
topics of interest to the sponsor. Sustaining sponsors fund content every year, whereas other 
sponsors fund content periodically. 
 
Emerging topics are questions about areas of interest to NCHS, CDC, or HHS. These are newer subject areas 
that have generally not been researched in the general population. 
 
 
 
Questionnaire Sections 
 
The NHIS is divided into many questionnaire sections within each module, each with a different focus. The sections 
may include any combination of annual core, rotating core, sponsored content, or emerging topics. When the same 
questions or same types of questions are asked in Sample Adult and Sample Child interviews, the sections are given 
the same name for both interviews.  
 
The names of the questions asked of the Sample Adult or pertaining to the Sample Adult’s family all end in “_A” 
whereas those asked of the Sample Child or about the Sample Child’s family end with “_C.” Section names have a 3-
letter abbreviation (e.g., INS for Health Insurance), and questions are grouped by module and section. 
 
 
 
Summary of the 2025 Content 
 
A description of the 2025 topics and type of questions are described in this report under Sample Adult’s Health, 
Sample Child’s Health, and Health Insurance, while all demographic information has been portioned into four 
sets of characteristics: 1) those about the Sample Adult and Sample Child; 2) those about the parents or 
guardian residing in the household with the Sample Child; 3) those about the spouse or partner residing in the 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 22 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
household with the Sample Adult (if married or cohabiting); and 4) those about the family and household of the 
Sample Adult and Sample Child. In this document, multiple questionnaire sections with annual, rotating, 
emerging and sponsored content are described in each of the health topics included under Sample Adult’s 
Health, Sample Child’s Health, and sets of demographic characteristics. 
 
 
 
Sample Adult health topics for 2025 
 
I. Health Status and Conditions 
self-reported health status, height, weight, pregnancy status, and the following health conditions: angina 
pectoris, anxiety disorder, arthritis, rheumatoid arthritis, gout, lupus, or fibromyalgia, asthma, attention 
deficit or hyperactivity disorder, cancer and cancer type, chronic fatigue syndrome, communications 
disorders, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, coronary heart disease, dementia including Alzheimer’s 
disease, depression, diabetes and type, epilepsy, gestational diabetes, hyperlipidemia, hypertension, 
immunosuppression, history of COVID and Long COVID, live birth in the past 12 months, menopausal 
symptoms and oophorectomy history, myocardial infarction, pre-diabetes, stroke, and whole person 
health. It also includes medication use for hyperlipidemia, hypertension, and diabetes including insulin 
initiation and GLP-1 injectables, emergency care for asthma, whether Long COVID symptoms limited usual 
activities, and whether overwhelmed by demands of living with diabetes. 
 
II. Functioning and Disability 
anxiety, cognition, communication and use of sign language, depression, hearing, mobility, self-care and 
upper body, social functioning (participation), vision, and age of disability onset. 
 
III. Chronic Pain 
frequency and severity of pain, pain impact, pain management techniques, pain in various body locations: 
back; hands, arms, or shoulders; hips, knees, or feet; headache or migraine; abdominal, pelvic, or genital 
pain; and toothache or jaw pain. 
 
IV. Health Care Access and Health Service Utilization 
Caregiving; difficulty paying for health care; health care use— medical care and wellness visit, dental 
care, mental health counseling, eye specialist, physical, speech, rehabilitate or occupational therapy, 
emergency care, hospitalization, retail health clinic, and urgent care center; immunizations (COVID-19, 
flu, pneumonia, RSV, shingles and Tdap); internet access and use of the internet for health and 
medical purposes; prescription medication; telehealth; usual place for medical care; unmet medical 
needs (dental, medical, mental health, and prescriptions) due to cost.  
 
V. Preventive Care and Services 
aspirin use; breast exam by a doctor, clinical checks for arterial blood pressure, blood glucose, A1C level, and 
cholesterol; history of hysterectomy; screenings for breast, cervical, colorectal, and prostate cancer; main 
reason for breast exam, colonoscopy, mammogram, PSA test, and for no up-to-date cervical and breast 
screening; received information about dense breast tissue at last mammogram; told if had PAP or HPV test 
at last cervical screening; payment for colonoscopy; a; doctor or health care professional recommended 
colorectal screening if no up-to-date colorectal screening; and number of PSA tests.  
 
VI. Health-Related Behaviors 
cigarette smoking; electronic cigarette use; use of other tobacco products (cigars and smokeless tobacco).  
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 23 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
VII. Mental Health 
General Anxiety Disorder-7 (GAD-7); Patient Health Questionnaire-8 (PHQ-8); life satisfaction; social parenting 
support; and social isolation and connectiveness. 
 
VIII. Injuries  
traumatic brain Injury. 
 
IX. Community Engagement and Social Context 
language spoken at home; participation in local elections and public meetings; and volunteer activities.  
 
 
 
Sample Child health topics for 2025 
 
I. Health Status and Conditions 
reported health status of the Sample Child, and the following health conditions: asthma, communication 
disorders, developmental conditions (Autism, Asperger’s disorder, pervasive development disorder, 
autism spectrum disorder, attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, intellectual disability, and learning 
disabilities), prediabetes or diabetes, history of COVID and Long COVID.  It also includes emergency care 
for asthma. 
 
II. Functioning and Disability 
anxiety, behavior, cognition, communication, depression, hearing, mobility, self-care and upper body, and 
vision. 
 
III. Health Care Access and Health Service Utilization 
difficulty paying for health care; health care use— medical care and wellness visit, dental care, mental 
health counseling, eye specialist, physical, speech, rehabilitate or occupational therapy, emergency care, 
hospitalization, retail health clinic, and urgent care center; immunizations (COVID-19, and flu); 
prescription medication; telehealth; usual place for medical care; and unmet medical needs (dental, 
medical, mental health, and prescriptions) due to cost.  
 
IV. Behavioral and Mental health 
Baby Pediatric Symptom Checklist (BPSC), and Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire (SDQ). 
 
V. Stressful Life Events 
had a parent who was incarcerated after birth, lived with someone with a mental illness, lived with 
someone with a drug or alcohol problem, and experienced or witnessed neighborhood violence.   
 
VI. Injuries 
traumatic brain injury.  
 
 
 
Health insurance  
coverage status at the time of interview, continuity and type of coverage in the past year, sources of 
coverage, characteristics of coverage, and reasons for no coverage.

and sex.  
Adult: education level, and currently in school; employment; marital status; parental status; sexual

IV. Sponsors 
 
Some 2025 NHIS content is sponsored by other federal agencies or other centers within CDC. . Sponsored content 
may be used to collect data on new topics or to go into more depth about subjects already on the NHIS. Sustaining 
sponsors add content every year. Other sponsors add content for selected years.

The National Center for Chronic Disease Prevention and Health Promotion at the Centers for Disease Control and 
Prevention (CDC/NCCDPHP) sponsored four Sample Adult questions on social connectivity and isolation. 
 
 
Social Support and Loneliness 
 
The National Center for Chronic Disease Prevention and Health Promotion at the Centers for Disease Control 
and Prevention (CDC/NCCDPHP) sponsored two Sample Adult questions on getting needed social support and 
feeling lonely. 
 
 
Traumatic Brain Injury (TBI) -Concussion 
 
The National Center for Injury Prevention and Control at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 
(CDC/NCIPC) sponsored five Sample Adult and five Sample Child questions on traumatic brain injury (concussion). 
 
 
Whole Person Health 
 
The National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health at the National Institutes of Health (NIH/NCCIH) 
sponsored eight Sample Adult questions asking respondents to rate different areas of their life.

V. Sample Sizes and Response Rates 
 
When the NHIS sample was redesigned for 2025, the goal was to produce about 30,000 Sample Adult 
interviews a year, with expected adult completion rates by state used to allocate the annual sample. This would 
require about 34,500 households with completed rosters, which, in turn, would yield about 8,000 Sample Child 
interviews. A total of 66,935 addresses were sampled for 2025; this number also took into account expected 
ineligible addresses (e.g., vacant, demolished, converted to a permanent business).  
 
Table 1 provides a breakdown of sample sizes for the various components of the 2025 NHIS public-use data 
release. As discussed in the section on data collection, the final 2025 sample sizes and response rates were 
impacted by the government shut down and the limitations on FR travel, hiring and retention.  
 
 
Table 1. Final sample sizes for the 2025 NHIS public-use data release 
Interview Unit 2025 Sample 
Households 25,182 
Sample Adults* 24,215 
Sample Children 6,049 
* For 465 of the 24,215 Sample Adults on the 2025 sample, a knowledgeable proxy answered for the Sample Adult 
because he or she was mentally or physically incapable of answering for himself or herself. 
 
 
 
Response Rate Method 
 
Beginning with the 2025 sample redesign, which supports state-level inference using at least three years of data, 
unweighted and weighted response rates are reported for the NHIS. According to AAPOR Standard Definitions 
(2023), surveys such as the NHIS that use complex, multi-stage sample designs with unequal probabilities of 
selection should report both weighted and unweighted response rates. Unweighted response rates are useful 
operationally because they provide a straightforward indicator of data collection performance and fieldwork 
success, allowing for comparisons of response performance across regions, FRs, or time periods. Weighted 
response rates better reflect the proportion of the target population represented by respondents and are 
particularly useful for evaluating the potential for nonresponse bias in survey estimates (Federal Committee on 
Statistical Methodology, 2001; Office of Management and Budget, 2006).  
 
The weighted household response and Household Roster Completion rates presented below utilize the household 
base weight, which reflects the inverse of the household probability of selection. Weighted conditional and 
unconditional Sample Adult and Sample Child response rates utilize the Sample Adult base weight and Sample Child 
base weight, respectively. The Sample Adult base weight is defined as: household base weight multiplied by the 
inverse of the Sample Adult probability of selection. Similarly, the Sample Child base weight is defined as: 
household base weight multiplied by the inverse of the Sample Child probability of selection.    
 
Unweighted and weighted response rates presented below conform to the American Association of Public Opinion 
Research (AAPOR) Response Rate Definition # 2, or AAPOR RR2 (AAPOR, 2023). “Interviewed Households,” 
“interviewed Sample Adults,” and “interviewed Sample Children” include those with completed interviews or 
acceptable “sufficient partial” interviews. 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 29 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
In the NHIS, a Sample Adult or Sample Child interview is considered fully complete when respondents complete 
all sections, and therefore questions, for which they are eligible. Conversely, an interview is considered a 
“partial” when all sections are not completed. The most common reason for a partial is a “break-off,” which 
occurs when a respondent stops the interview in-progress before completion and the FR fails to complete the 
interview during the allotted assignment period. The partial interview rate is the percent of all sufficiently 
complete interviews that are not entirely complete. These “sufficient partials” are counted as interviews in the 
computation of response rates. Partials that are not far enough along in the interview, known as “insufficient 
partials,” are considered refusals and therefore included as eligible, non-respondents in the computation of 
response rates. 
 
 
 
2025 Sample 
 
Household Response Rates 
 
For the 2025 Sample, the weighted household response rate was calculated by dividing the weighted number of 
interviewed households (N=56,824,682) by the sum of the weighted number of interviewed households 
(N=56,824,682) and the weighted number of nonresponding households (N=71,369,304). Nonresponding 
households are eligible households that were not interviewed for a variety of reasons, including language barriers, 
no one home after repeated contact attempts, refusal, household records rejected for insufficient data, or other 
reasons for no interview. Calculation of the unweighted household response rate follows the same logic: number of 
interviewed households (n=25,182) divided by the sum of the number of interviewed households (n=25,182) and 
the number of nonresponding households (n=32,420). 
 
The weighted Household Response Rate for the 2025 Sample was 44.3%. 
 
The unweighted Household Response Rate for the 2025 Sample was 43.7%. 
 
The weighted Household Response Rate should be reported if one is performing a household-level analysis using 
the public-use paradata file. 
 
 
 
Household Roster Completion 
 
The completion of the Household Roster is defined as the enumeration of all persons in an eligible household, with 
basic demographic information collected about each household member. The weighted Household Roster 
Completion Rate is calculated by dividing the weighted number of eligible households with a completed Household 
Roster (N=62,845,485) by the weighted number of eligible households (N=128,193,986). For the 2025 Sample, the 
weighted Household Roster Completion Rate was 49.0%. The unweighted Household Roster Completion Rate was 
48.4%: number of eligible households with a completed Household Roster (n=27,867) divided by the number of 
eligible households (n=57,602). Based on demographic information obtained from completed Household Rosters, 
there were 7,167 (weighted=29,707,804) eligible Sample Children and 27,831 (weighted=119,040,844) eligible 
Sample Adults in the 2025 Sample.  
 
 
 
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 30 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Sample Child Response Rates 
 
Sample Child response rates can be computed two ways, resulting in either a conditional or final response rate. 
The weighted Conditional Sample Child Response Rate is calculated by dividing the weighted number of 
interviewed Sample Children (N=25,433,474) by the weighted number of eligible Sample Children from 
households with completed rosters (N=29,707,804). For the 2025 Sample, the weighted Conditional Sample 
Child Response Rate was 85.6%. The unweighted Conditional Sample Child Response rate was 84.4%: number 
of interviewed Sample Children (n=6,049) divided by the number of eligible Sample Children from households 
with completed rosters (n=7,167). 
 
The weighted Final Sample Child Response Rate accounts for the weighted Household Roster Completion Rate 
and is calculated by dividing the weighted number of interviewed Sample Children (N=25,433,474) by the 
weighted number of eligible Sample Children (N=29,707,804) from households with completed rosters, and then 
multiplying this quotient by the weighted Household Roster Completion Rate (49.0%). Here again, the 
unweighted Final Sample Child Response Rate follows the same logic: number of interviewed Sample Children 
(n=6,049) divided by the number of eligible Sample Children from households with completed rosters (n=7,167) 
multiplied by the unweighted Household Roster Completion Rate (48.4%). In 2025, 1.4% (weighted=1.5%) of 
Sample Child interviews were sufficient partials. 
 
The weighted Final Sample Child Response Rate for the 2025 Sample was 42.0%. 
The unweighted Final Sample Child Response Rate for the 2025 Sample was 40.8%. 
 
Data users should report the weighted Final Sample Child Response Rate when reporting on analyses performed 
with the Sample Child data file.  
 
 
 
Sample Adult Response Rates 
 
As with Sample Children, both a conditional and final response rate can be computed for Sample Adults. The 
weighted Conditional Sample Adult Response Rate is calculated by dividing the weighted number of interviewed 
Sample Adults (N=100,307,488) by the weighted number of eligible Sample Adults from households with completed 
rosters (N=119,040,844). For the 2025 Sample, the weighted Conditional Sample Adult Response Rate was 84.3%. 
The unweighted Conditional Sample Adult Response rate was 87.0%: number of interviewed Sample Adults 
(n=24,215) divided by the number of eligible Sample Adults from households with completed rosters (n=27,831). 
 
The weighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate is calculated by dividing the weighted number of interviewed 
Sample Adults (N=100,307,488) by the weighted number of eligible Sample Adults from households with completed 
rosters (N=119,040,844) and then multiplying this quotient by the weighted Household Roster Completion Rate 
(49.0%).  The unweighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate uses the same formula: number of interviewed Sample 
Adults (n=24,215) divided by the number of eligible Sample Adults from households with completed rosters 
(n=27,831) multiplied by the unweighted Household Roster Completion Rate (48.4%). In the 2025 Sample, 3.4% 
(weighted=3.4%) of Sample Adult interviews were sufficient partials. 
 
The weighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate for the 2025 sample was 41.3%. 
The unweighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate for the 2025 sample was 42.1%. 
 
Data users should report the weighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate when reporting on analyses performed 
with the Sample Adult data file.  
 
2025 National Health Interview Survey (NHIS) Survey Description    Page 31 
 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services ● Centers for Disease Control and Prevention ● National Center for Health Statistics 
Note that unweighted and weighted numbers of households, Sample Children, and Sample Adults eligible and 
interviewed were used for the calculations of response rates shown and rounding discrepancies may occur 
when using the percentages. 
 
 
 
Reporting Household, Sample Adult, and Sample Child Response Rates 
 
As noted above, data users should report the weighted Final Sample Adult Response Rate when reporting on 
analyses performed with the Sample Adult data file. Similarly, data users should report the weighted Final Sample 
Child Response Rate on analyses performed with the Sample Child data file. The weighted Household Response Rate 
should be reported if one is performing a household-level analysis using the public-use paradata file. The weighted 
Household Roster Completion Rate and the weighted Conditional Response Rates may be reported, in addition to 
weighted Final Response Rates, when data users want to include additional information about the effectiveness of 
each stage of the data collection process. If one is analyzing multiple years of data, say 2024 and 2025, Tables 2–5 
provide weighted and unweighted counts and response rates from 2019–2025. 
 
Response rates obtained during 1997–2018 NHIS and 2019–2025 NHIS should be presented separately. First, 
the definition of an interviewed household differs from the past questionnaire design (1997–2018). Previously, 
an interviewed household was defined as one where at least one family in the household completed a 
substantial portion of the family interview. With the family interview removed from the redesigned NHIS, an 
interviewed household is now defined as one where the Household Roster and a substantial portion of either 
the Sample Adult interview or the Sample Child interview (if one or more children reside in the household) is 
completed. In addition, Final Sample Adult and Sample Child Response Rates during 1997–2018 NHIS were the 
product of the Conditional Sample Adult or Sample Child Response Rate and the Final Family Response Rate. 
Starting in 2019, Final Sample Adult and Sample Child Response Rates are the product of the Conditional Sample 
Adult or Sample Child Response Rate and the Household Roster Completion Rate.
